ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u1	Dear fellow Members, distinguished fellow Members, ladies and gentlemen! I'm about to proceed with the 12th session of the National Assembly. At the end of the day, this is a second defensive proposal for a law on public assembly within the framework of the ordinary procedure. The draft law was submitted to the National Assembly by a group of 81 Members and Members with a first-signed magi. Diane the Jew. In order to further explain the bill, I give the floor to Mrs Tina Heferle's representative. Madam Vice President, please.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u2	Mr President, thank you for the floor. The institution, as a term, is a social structure that represents certain rules for the behaviour or functioning of a larger group of people and, as such, key state authorities, parliament, government, courts are also established in most countries around the world. In our country, the National Assembly is one of the few state institutions that has no special law for its operation, such as the President of the Republic, the Council of State, the Court of Auditors, the Constitutional Court, the Ombudsman, the Government and so on. The importance and role of the institution, which is the only legislator in our country, is not particularly clear, and the fact is that the constitutional position clearly points to the autonomy of the National Assembly, whose starting point is the ability of the Authority to design and adopt rules for its functioning, and the possibility of autonomous decision-making on the use of the resources necessary for the effective exercise of its powers. The unfortunate comparison of the Law on the National Assembly with the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, which is a procedural act governing the legislative procedure in particular, and the defence of their mutual exclusion is attributed to the lack of understanding of the nature of the constitutional provisions. The Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia regulates the organisation and functioning of the National Assembly quite succinctly, resulting in the possibility of a relatively high degree of autonomy in the regulation of its functioning to this highest legislative institution. The autonomy enshrined in the constitutional provision by the Law on the National Assembly must be understood as the independence and non-subordination of the National Assembly to the executive branch of power and, on the other hand, this autonomy for the National Assembly constitutes a restriction to act under certain predetermined rules. It is not to be forgotten that the National Assembly is not only an institution in which Members and Members operate, but also services that provide professional, technical and administrative support. Their status and organisation at the legal level are not regulated, and the law on the National Assembly is also necessary. The basic objective of the proposal for a Law on the National Assembly is to ensure that the National Assembly has financial, administrative and security autonomy, clearly defined the status of the Secretary-General, legally defined rules on the protection of personal and other data and their processing, the basis for regulating the functioning of the services and the specificities of certain labour law issues, the protection of the National Assembly, the conduct of house investigations at the premises of the National Assembly, international cooperation and other matters to ensure a comprehensive legal regulation of the functioning of the National Assembly as an institution. These are the substantive reasons why Members who have tabled a proposal for a law have decided and we believe that an institution such as the National Assembly needs its own law to regulate more specifically what neither the Constitution nor the Rules of Procedure prescribe. Or, in other words, if an institution as important as the National Assembly does not have its own law, while, at the outset, I have mentioned it, some state authorities have it, it points to an unequal or even subordinate position of the National Assembly in state order, which is of course not good. Although this law has not received as much support as the National Assembly, as an institution, Members as its representatives and professional and technical staff who carry out their work in this institution, we deserve it, but we have achieved a broad political consensus among both the coalitions and the opposition here and have proposed the adoption of a law that we should have adopted a few years ago. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u9	Thank you, Member, for presenting your position. Mr Igor Peček on behalf of the Group of Ambassadors of the List of Marjan Šarc. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u10	Thank you very much. Dear President, distinguished representatives of the government, colleagues and colleagues! We are co-signers of the proposed law in the Marjan Sarca Group of Members, and we will support the law today. I would not like to repeat the substantive solutions that the law brings, and we have already heard about this from the representative of the proposalr, but I would like to highlight the key aspect for which we in the LMŠ Group estimate to justify or even more, requires the adoption of legislation in the National Assembly. That's autonomy. The autonomy of Parliament as an institution based on the principle of the division of power is defined in the majority of the constitutions of European countries. While autonomy depends on social, democratic and political culture, in some countries parliaments face considerable dependence on the executive branch of government. This creates a state policy and Parliament serves as a tool for adopting the necessary rules. This reduces the independence of the legislative branch of government. Parliament's autonomy is therefore a matter for the representative body to exercise its legislative function independently and independently and has the competence to adopt its own rules under which it operates. However, Parliament's autonomy does not give special rights if we compare the legislative branch with enforcement or judicial authority. Parliament's autonomy is therefore limited and permissible only to a level that takes into account the specificity of the organisation and the work of Parliament and prevents other branches of government from influencing the efficiency and quality of the functioning of the legislative branch of authority. Parliament can thus determine the organisation of work itself, but in the framework of this autonomy it must not fall into the work of other state bodies which have their own constitutionally protected position. And, of course, the latter also apply in the reverse situation, when neither the Government nor the judiciary can interfere with the legislative branch of power. The National Assembly is constitutionally viewed by the Government as an equivalent body whose independence and independence must also extend to a certain extent to the budgetary area. The financial autonomy of the National Assembly derives from its position in the system of organisation of authorities, which is not defined anywhere at the normative level. The main issue of the financial autonomy of the National Assembly is in particular the degree of independence and independence it is guaranteed in the composition of its financial plan and the degree of autonomy it has in its implementation during the financial year. Financial autonomy follows, therefore, the provisions of Article 8 of the Constitutional Court Act and does not bring innovation at the legal system level, reflecting one of the most important budgetary principles already in force, which is the principles of the prior approval of the budget, which implies that the budget can only be implemented once it has been adopted by the representative body. Also, the proposed provision relating to the supervision of the Court of Auditors over the use of parliamentary budget funds is not new at legal level and represents only consistent respect for the principle of the division of power, whereby control over the expenditure of the budget of the National Assembly can only be exercised by an independent and independent body such as the Court of Auditors and not by the Government. If I withdraw, Parliamentary autonomy, which is regulated at the legal level by today's proposal, thus covers regulatory, administrative and financial autonomy and autonomy in ensuring security. The purpose of the law is to clearly define the status of employees and the basis for the functioning of the services of the National Assembly, which will be adapted to its specific nature of work. As we have already heard today, both the Government and, say, the Constitutional Court and the Court of Auditors have their own law, and the Government also have their own rules of procedure, so in our opinion it is not unusual for us to adopt law also for the legislative branch of power. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u12	Thank you, President, for your word. Dear colleagues and colleagues! The proposed Law on the National Assembly aims to ensure that administrative, financial and security autonomy is administered to the National Assembly as the highest institution of the legislative branch of power at the legal level. Autonomy means the ability of the National Assembly to draw up and adopt its own rules and to provide the resources necessary for the effective exercise of its powers. The National Assembly shall confirm the terms of office of Members, decide on the immunity of Members, Judges of the Constitutional Court and Judges and the Ombudsman. However, the powers of the National Assembly are not limited to the adoption of laws and other legal acts; it is an extremely important task of the National Assembly as part of the electoral function to elect, appoint and resolve the most important function holders in the National Assembly, the Government, the Constitutional Court and in accordance with the Constitution and laws in other institutions. In addition to the legislative work, it also decides to declare a state of war and emergency and to use the defence forces. In the framework of the supervisory function, the National Assembly shall determine the parliamentary inquiry, decide on the confidential and non-confidential nature of the government, decide on the charges of the President of the Republic, the Prime Minister and the Ministers before the Constitutional Court, raise questions of Members and initiatives to the President of the Government, the Ministers and the Secretary-General of the Government. The autonomy of the National Assembly is shown by the fact that it is not subordinated to other national authorities in its work and in the performance of its functions, and in particular to the executive branch, since it actually has the greatest power in the state organisation. In the case of financial autonomy, it means that the financial resources for the smooth functioning of the performance of the tasks shall be determined independently and the Court of Auditors of the Republic of Slovenia shall exercise control over the use of the financial resources. In its opinion, the government stressed that the responsibility for achieving fiscal stability and sustainable and sustainable national economic development was shared with the Government. But don't worry, we're all aware of that. At the time of adoption of the budget resources, it is constantly weighing to whom and how much, for social, health, education and education, culture, science, state security, infrastructure, investment and could also be listed. For the well-being of individuals and countries, last but not least, each segment is important. Whether we admit it or not, the work in the National Assembly is specific. It is necessary to organise the work of 90 elected representatives of the people and employees of public servants who are responsible for the smooth functioning of this highest state institution. The professional, technical and administrative support to the National Assembly at the legal level has so far not been regulated. However, the adoption of the Law on the National Assembly will mean that we have a basis for ensuring consistent administrative autonomy of the National Assembly, which would allow the National Assembly to act on its own rules and not only by general rules or that it is autonomous in its internal affairs. And if I just get attached here, it's just the way it's going. If we, Members and Members of Parliament, are considered to be serving 24 hours a day, seven days a week or 365 days a year, we cannot say that for other public servants of the National Assembly and the parliamentary groups. Their work obligation is linked to the work of us, Members and Members when we are here, and I can ensure that, as a rule, it is not limited to eight workers as it is to other public servants who work between 8 and 16 hours. It is therefore right that the leadership of the National Assembly, which takes care of the smooth progress of the work processes, has the opportunity to regulate this by means of internal rules. The complete security autonomy of the National Assembly began to be considered primarily in the context of house investigations in the National Assembly. ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u17	Thank you, Member, for presenting your position. Mr Blaž Pavlin on behalf of the New Slovenia Group – Christian Democrats. Here you go, Mr. Ambassador.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u18	Dear President, Representative of the Government, colleague and fellow Members! The proposed Act on the National Assembly, which we are considering today, is the first law in Slovenia, which regulates the organisation and functioning of the National Assembly in its entirety. Unlike the National Assembly, the operation of most of the highest authorities in the Republic of Slovenia is regulated by law. Thus we have the Law on the State Council, the Law on the provision of conditions for the performance of the presidency of the Republic, the Law on the Government of the Republic of Slovenia, the Law on the Bank of Slovenia, the Law on the Court of Auditors and the Law on the Constitutional Court. The functioning of the National Assembly is now primarily governed by the Rules of Procedure, which does not address all the issues and specificities required by the nature of the work of the National Assembly. The proposed law is the basis for the consistent provision of the financial, administrative and security autonomy of the National Assembly, a clear definition of the status of the Secretary-General, a basis for regulating the functioning of the services of the National Assembly, specificities and certain labour law issues, the protection of the National Assembly, international cooperation and other matters, thus ensuring a comprehensive legal regulation of the functioning of the National Assembly as an institution. Autonomy is not only intended to formalise the principle of equality between national authorities, but is a necessity for the exercise of constitutional powers and the function of the National Assembly and for the effective functioning of the political system. The National Assembly must choose autonomy primarily in relation to the executive power, which is actually the greatest power in the country. In particular, I would like to draw attention to the two main solutions brought by the proposed law. The first relates to the resources for the functioning of the National Assembly, which are determined independently by the National Assembly and are an integral part of the budget of the Republic of Slovenia. The amount of funds shall be determined by allowing the exercise of the powers and the functioning of the National Assembly. The use of funds shall be decided by the National Assembly on its own; supervision of the use of funds shall be exercised by the Court of Auditors. The second relates to the entry of investigative bodies carrying out judicial orders at the premises of the National Assembly. This area has not been regulated so far. In the new case, the court which ordered the investigation will have to inform the President of the National Assembly prior to the conduct of the house investigation or the investigation of electronic and related devices and electronic data carriers at the premises of the National Assembly. In the course of the investigation, a representative of the National Assembly may be present, and in the course of the investigation at the premises of the parliamentary group, the investigating commission or the working body may also be present at the premises of the said parliamentary group or the working body in which the investigation is conducted. Members of New Slovenia will support the bill.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u19	Thank you, Member, for presenting your Group's position. Mag. Andrej Reich on behalf of the Party's Group Alenka Bratušek. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u20	Thank you so much for saying that. Dear colleagues and colleagues! Article 3 of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia sets out the principle of the division of power, meaning that in our country power is divided into legislative, executive and judicial. The holder of the legislative branch of power is the National Assembly, one of the few most important national authorities, which is still not regulated at the legal level. While the Constitution provides for the adoption by the National Assembly of rules of procedure governing its functioning and competence, the adoption of the Rules of Procedure requires a two-thirds majority of Members' votes. In this context, the Constitution does not prohibit the adoption of a law regulating the organisation and operation of the National Assembly. In fact, it is a matter of concreteising constitutional provisions. However, the provisions of the bill are without prejudice to the content governed by the Rules of Procedure, as the Legislative and Legal Service has also found in its opinion. As outlined in the bill, its main objective is to ensure autonomy and independence for the National Assembly. This is an equivalent body to the Government, which cannot be subordinated to other state authorities. The Government has put forward certain concerns, particularly regarding the financial autonomy of the National Assembly as set out in the proposal. The main purpose of the provision is to ensure that the National Assembly is not subordinate to the Government and is thus able to carry out its work smoothly. As a result, the National Assembly will prepare the draft budget on its own and decide on the use of these funds, which does not, of course, mean that it can arbitrarily and arbitrarily determine the extent of the funds, as it must respect public financial sustainability. This is not about introducing specificities, as is illustrated by the fact that quite a few Member States of the European Union know such arrangements. The power to control the use of funds will be conferred on the Court of Auditors. In addition to financial autonomy, the proposal provides for administrative and security autonomy. The protection of the institution remains within the purview of the Police, which must comply with the instructions of the College of the President of the National Assembly. Provision shall also be made for the conduct of a house investigation at the premises of the National Assembly. This is a matter which has already been dealt with by the Constitutional Court, since in these cases it is not merely an intervention in the right to privacy of individuals. That is why special protection is provided for Members in such investigations. The status and powers of the Secretary-General of the National Assembly, which is given the status of an official and the power to propose a financial plan, are also being regulated. The proposal further refers to the organisation of the services of the National Assembly, in particular the parliamentary groups. These are public servants who provide professional and administrative assistance in the parliamentary groups. The services of the parliamentary groups are subject to certain specifics as the proposal for a law in Article 15 provides that the working relationship in the service of the parliamentary group is linked to the personal trust of Members. For this reason, such employment contracts may only be concluded for a certain period of time and without a public tender. Among other things, at the legal level, personal data processing and specificities relating to the business and working time of the National Assembly shall also be regulated. At the same time, work is defined in the event of a state of war or emergency and the right to strike employees. The proposal lays down the tasks that public servants are obliged to perform in the case of a sentence. The law was created under the watchful eye of the Legislative and Legal Service, whose comments we Members took into account at the time of the drafting of the regulation. We believe that the bill is well-prepared and will contribute to the quality exercise of the powers of the National Assembly. We will unanimously support the bill in the SAB Group. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u22	Thank you for your word, Madam President. Dear Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues! The Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia states in the second paragraph of Article 3: “In Slovenia the people have power. Citizens and citizens shall execute it directly and by elections, in accordance with the principle of division of power into legislative, executive and judicial.” In Slovenia, power is divided into legislative, executive and judicial. Each branch of power shall have its own responsibilities and its own responsibilities, one of which shall be independent and independent, and none shall interfere with the work of the other branches of authority. But they control each other and they limit each other. The holder of the executive branch of power is the Government of the Republic of Slovenia, whose composition, work and organization are, inter alia, defined in a separate law, the Law on the Government of the Republic of Slovenia. The institution of the judiciary is the courts whose composition, work and organisation are also determined by specific legislation. The institution of the legislative branch of power, legislation, is Parliament, the National Assembly of the Republic of Slovenia, but unfortunately we cannot continue here at this time in terms of: whose composition, work and organisation are determined by special law. In the Desus Group, we expect and hope that today is a turning point in this regard. In fact, Parliament, as one of the branches of power in the Republic of Slovenia, does not have its structure legally regulated in one place, but is subject to the composition, work and organisation of this institution, both at constitutional, legal and sub-legal levels. Indeed, our Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly represent a constitution in a small and comprehensive manner governing the organisation and work of the National Assembly, but we are convinced by Members of the Desus Group that as an institution, the National Assembly already requires autonomous and autonomous legislation, as it is regulated for other state bodies. For example: the President of the Republic of Slovenia, the State Council, the Ombudsman, the Court of Auditors, the Information Officer and others. We must admit that this idea is by no means new to this mandate. Intensions after the adoption of the independent Law on the National Assembly have been present virtually since the independence of the Council, but unfortunately there has never been sufficient political will to actually approach the formation and introduction of a roof law. This time, we have united Members and Members of Parliament irrespective of our political affiliation and formed a working group whose result is the text of the law that we have before us. Together, we can all be proud of the fact that from now on the Slovenian Parliament will take sides with other European countries, which have been properly regulated for decades. The proposed Act on the National Assembly in one place governs the financial, administrative, security autonomy of the whole institution, the status of the Secretary-General and the framework for the operation of the services, and other matters which jointly ensure the comprehensive legislative arrangements of the legislative body. ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u32	Thank you for your word, President. Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members! I will present you with a proposal for a law amending the Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Medical Service Act, an urgent procedure for which we ask you to support it. Until 2017, funds for the implementation of the traineeship of all healthcare professionals and healthcare professionals were provided from the funds of the Institute of Health Insurance of Slovenia, i.e. from the means of compulsory health insurance. By adopting the Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Medical Service Act, hereinafter the ZZdrS-F, which was adopted in July 2017, it was stipulated that these funds are provided from the budget of the Republic of Slovenia. For financial reasons, the transfer of the obligation to provide funds to the budget was gradual. The dynamics of the gradual financing of those budgeted content are set out in Article 26 of the ZZdrS-F, from EUR 23 million in 2017 to EUR 80 million in 2020, with the difference to the overall value being provided by the ZZS. Of which, in 2021, all funds shall be fully provided from the budget. During the implementation of the ZZdrS-F, it was found that the level of the budget for 2020 is limited to €80 million and the estimated level of funding for traineeships and specialisations is €91.7 million, with no more guaranteed coverage of the difference to the total value of funding for these content. The assessment of the level of resources required is higher each year due to wage increases in the public sector as well as an increase in the number of trainees and specialists, which is also the aim of the ministry. Funding for traineeships and specialisations is now the largest part of the budget of the Ministry of Health and it is therefore necessary to adopt the amendment of the ZZdrS-F by extending the period of provision of a difference to the full value of the funding from the ZZS to 31. 12. 2020. At the same time, it shall be extended mutatis mutandis to 31. 12. 2020 also the validity of other provisions of that Article relating to the recovery of funds in the event of unfinished specialisation or non-employment in the public health network, namely, by the end of 2020 those provisions of the CFSP and the determination of the level of the means of specialisation and a more detailed description of the rights by the end of 2020, in agreement with the CFSP, then the Ministry itself. In view of the favourable financial situation this year, we propose that, for 2019, more than €10 million be earmarked for the financing of traineeships and specialisations from the budget than provided for by the law in force, a total of €70 million. For 2019, it has already been estimated that the amount of funding for traineeships and specialisations will amount to €85.5 million and €60 million from the budget. By increasing the level of resources from the budget, we will ensure a smaller participation of the ZZS in the difference up to the full value, which means more funding for health services and reduction of waiting periods. The appropriations are already provided in the corresponding budget item — transfer from the second item. Therefore, by amending the law, we extend the period of ensuring the difference to the full value of traineeship financing and specialisations from CFSP funds to 31. 12. 2020, to ensure the continued implementation of these contents, and at the same time this year, €10 million will be increased by funds to finance these contents from the budget, thus depriving compulsory health insurance funds, which means more financing for health services and reducing waiting periods. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u38	Thank you so much for saying that. The Medical Service Act primarily lays down the conditions for the performance of the medical service, the private medical service, the rights and duties of doctors and the powers and duties of administrative authorities and public authorities. Among other things, the law provides for the financing of traineeships for healthcare professionals and co-workers of secondary and specialist doctors. We provide the means to carry out the traineeship of all those listed in the budget by gradually transferring funding from compulsory health insurance funds to the budget. Thus, this year, we allocate €60 million from the budget of the Republic of Slovenia and €80 in the coming year. The Institute of Health Insurance of Slovenia provides a difference up to the full value of the funding of the traineeship for the period until 31 December of this year. The Ministry of Health has concluded that for 2020 the estimated level of funding for traineeships for a good EUR 10 million exceeds the amount provided by the budget for this purpose. At the same time, the coverage of the difference by the Institute of Health Insurance of Slovenia is no longer ensured. That is why the proposed amendment of the law extends the period of ensuring a difference to the entire estimated value of the financing of traineeships under the CFSP until 31 December next year. In this way, we will avoid limiting the number of already approved traineeships. This would lead to difficult remedial consequences for the health system in the current absence of doctors and some other health workers. The Ministry also proposes to change the funding dynamics for 2019 by providing €70 million for the funding of traineeships in the budget in 2019, which is 10 million more than currently provided for in the Medical Services Act. This will provide an additional EUR 10 million from compulsory health insurance to reduce waiting periods. In the SAB Group, we will support the proposed change, and we welcome any solution that brings improvements to the health system and the shorter waiting line. Nevertheless, citizens who are conscientiously paying for medical bills rightly expect the available health resources to be distributed in a way that ensures the highest possible quality, efficiency and accountability in health care. ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u54	Madam President, once again, thank you for your word. Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members! I will present you with a proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Law on Medicinal Products following a reduced procedure, for which we ask you to support it. The proposal for a Law on Amendments and Supplements to the Medicinal Products Act, which is dealt with under shorter procedure, is made in three parts. The judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union in Medisanus of 8 June 2017 is being implemented. Secondly, the provisions of the Medicinal Products Act, hereinafter referred to as ZZdr-2, shall be brought into line with Directive 2011/62/EU and Commission Delegated Regulation (EU) 2016/161. Thirdly, a more precise definition for a biological medicinal product should be laid down in the procedure for authorisation of a medicinal product for human and veterinary use in accordance with Directive 2001/83/EC and Directive 2001/82/EC. In the context of the enforcement of the judgment of the EU Court of Justice in the Medisanus case, the EU Court of Justice in the Medisanus case against the General Hospital Murska Sobota of 8. Seven. 2017 ruled that in the procurement of plasma products in the tendering documentation it is not permitted to give priority to a medicinal product from plasma collected in the Republic of Slovenia, which consequently means that Article 71(6) ZZdr-2, which provides for the priority use of medicinal products from Slovenian plasma, does not comply with EU rules. In view of the above, Article 6(6) of the ZZdr-2 should be deleted in order to implement this judgment. The deletion of that point in Article 6 of the ZZdr-2 does not affect the principle of self-sufficiency, which provides, within the framework of the public service, the supply of medicinal products from its own plasma source, which is governed by the Blood Supply Act and the Decree of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia of 1. 3. 2011. That principle, as stated by the Court in its explanation of this judgment, is not contrary to EU rules, as it pursues legitimate public health objectives. Slovenia is working on this principle to provide medicinal products from its own plasma source to the maximum extent possible. On the basis of point 2, the harmonisation of the provisions of the ZZdr-2 in the part relating to the protection elements of medicinal products by Directive 2011/62/EU and Commission Delegated Regulation (EU) 2016/161. The proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Medicinal Products Act specifies in more detail the term of the protective element on the outer packaging of medicinal products for human use and aligns the provisions of the ZZdr-2 in the part relating to the protective elements of medicinal products with those of the EU. From 9 February 2019, manufacturers of medicinal products used in human medicine and subject to prescription must place on the outer packaging two protective elements that otherwise provide identification, traceability, authenticity and safety of the medicinal product. In particular, the identification, traceability and authenticity of the medicinal product is ensured by a two-dimensional line code, which is a protective element in the form of a record on the outer packaging of the individual medicinal product, and the safety of the medicinal product is ensured by an anti-invasion device which can be used to check whether the medicinal product has been physically affected. Both protective elements on the outer packaging together prevent the intrusion of counterfeit medicinal products into the legal distribution chain of medicinal products and ensure greater traceability of medicinal products and greater safety for end-users. That delegated Regulation also provides for exemptions to certain product groups from those obligations which do not have protective features. If a medicinal product does not have a marketing authorisation in any of the Member States of the European Union and we urgently need it in Slovenia, it should be imported by intervention from third countries which are bound by this delegated Regulation and do not have protective features. The Public Agency of the Republic of Slovenia for Medicines and Medical Devices shall verify their safety and authenticity by means of documentary documentation and verification of the liability of all business entities in the legal distribution chain. The amendment of the law is necessary for the implementation of this Delegated Regulation and for the definition of that exemption from the obligation primarily for medicinal products in the case of third-country intervention imports into the Republic of Slovenia which will not be equipped with protective elements. The proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Medicinal Products Act therefore, by deleting Article 71(6) of the ZZdr-2, executes the judgment of the Court in Medisanus of 8. Seven. 2017. The deletion of that point in no way impedes the implementation of the principle of self-sufficiency, which provides for the supply of medicinal products from its own plasma source within the public service, which is governed by the Blood Supply Act and the Decree of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia of 1. 3. 2011. Secondly, the provisions of the ZZdr-2 shall be aligned with the Directive and the Delegated Regulation on protective components of medicinal products for human use intended to ensure the identification, traceability, authenticity and safety of the medicinal product and to prevent the entry into the legal distribution chain of counterfeit medicinal products, while defining that exception in the case of emergency intervention imports. Thank you, President.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u60	Thank you so much for saying that, President. Say good-bye to everyone again! These medicines are owned by Slovenia, which supports hospitals and thus provides 60% of the necessary resources, and by 40% the hospitals choose the supplier of plasma medicinal products by themselves or by common contract. The novelty also brings some alignment with the European Directives governing the field of medicines. This provides for a more precise definition of the biological medicinal product in the marketing authorisation procedure, for use in human medicine and veterinary medicine, and identifies the protective elements on the outer packaging of the medicinal product. Since 9 February, manufacturers of medicinal products that are subject to medical prescription and are used in human medicine should place on the outer packaging two protective elements that together provide identification, traceability, authenticity and safety of the medicinal product. This is a two-dimensional line code and a device that protects against interference in the package. The novelty also regulates exemptions from those conditions relating to the intervention imports of medicinal products from third countries where this is necessary to ensure adequate supply of medicinal products in the country. Members of the Desus Group will support the proposal. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u66	Dear President, Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues! In addition, it is necessary to harmonise the law with a delegated regulation relating to the protective elements of medicinal products for human use. Furthermore, we are obliged to harmonise the definition of a biological medicinal product for human and veterinary use with the directives governing this area. The amendments to the Medicinal Products Act therefore relate to less difficult harmonisation of the law with European Union law. As mentioned, amendments to the law will follow the judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union in the Medisanus case. In early July 2017, the Court found that Article 71(6) of the current Act on Medicinal Products, which lays down the principle of the advantage of supply of industrially manufactured medicinal products from Slovenian plasma, is not in accordance with European Union law. In the procurement process, it is not allowed to give priority to a medicinal product from Slovenian plasma, so we would like to delete Article 71(6) by amending the law. In doing so, I should stress that the deletion of the point will not affect the principle of self-sufficiency, which will keep the Republic of Slovenia seeking to provide a medicinal product from Slovenian plasma to the maximum extent possible. By implementing the principle of self-sufficiency in the future, we will ensure that medicinal products from such pooled plasma are actually used to treat patients in Slovenia. Another important change concerns the more precise definition of the term “protective elements” on the external packaging of medicinal products used for treatment in human medicine. These prescription medicinal products should have two protective elements on the outer carton from the beginning of this year. The first in the form of a two-dimensional line code and the second in the form of a protective device against interference with the medicinal product. The objective of the protective elements is to ensure, in the right order, greater safety, easier identification, traceability and authenticity of medicinal products. In addition, the protective elements will help prevent counterfeit medicinal products from entering the legal distribution chain of medicinal products. I would point out that the novel also provides for exceptions when drugs do not have protective elements. This is particularly important in the case of medicinal products which will need to be imported by intervention from third countries, as the current law does not provide for an exemption. Furthermore, the amendment also provides for a more precise definition of a biological medicinal product in the procedure for obtaining a marketing authorisation for medicinal products for human use and veterinary medicinal products with the European Directives governing this area. We believe that the proposed amendments to the Law on Medicinal Products prepared by the Ministry of Health are appropriate and necessary, so we will support the amendment of the law. Thank you very much.

Agenda item — Third defence of the draft law on the procedure of judicial protection of former holders of qualifying obligations of banks under the ordinary procedure. The draft law was tabled by the Government. ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u76	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. Dear Members and Members! Third reading of the draft law on judicial and extrajudicial protection of former holders of qualified liabilities of banks is before you. The law is intended for judicial protection for the removal of subordinated bonds and bank shares on the basis of decisions of the Bank of Slovenia. Judicial protection on other grounds, such as a breach of an explanatory duty at the time of the sale of those instruments, was possible at all times. Some investors have also decided on compensation actions within the framework of the provisions of the Penal Code. Actions under this Act are brought against the Bank of Slovenia and, on other grounds, against business banks and their responsible persons. The proposal under consideration eliminates the inconsistencies identified by the Constitutional Court in relation to the effectiveness of investor justice. Therefore, in the drafting of the law and in the legislative process, we have consistently followed the key directions of the 2016 Constitutional Decision. So we predicted, we applied the following principles. First, to allow applicants access to all data relevant for the assessment of the correctness of the deletions and of the exceptional measures carried out, for which Banka Slovenije was authorised. Secondly, to establish the possibility of collective judicial protection. Thirdly, it was necessary to establish a reverse burden of proof on the Bank of Slovenia, which means that the Central Bank must demonstrate that it correctly applied the legal provisions and correctly calculated the capital deficit in banks receiving State aid. It was also necessary to establish procedural provisions which, in a constitutionally consistent manner and without retroactive interference with the legal bases in force in 2013 and 2014, would allow effective judicial protection for former holders. This is what the proposed law now provides. We have not introduced into the law the requirements of former investors to intervene retroactively in material provisions regarding procedures, a careful review of banks, in the valuation methodology and in the application of accounting standards. These issues will have to be clarified in court proceedings. It is not irrelevant that the Bank of Slovenia carried out exceptional measures before the implementation of the single European bank resolution mechanism. Therefore, the verification of the normative basis and the procedures by which the valuations of the bank portfolio were carried out will be a key issue on the basis of which the court will assess the compatibility of the measures carried out with the then applicable normative basis. We have also not been able to resolve the questions of Banka Slovenije's responsibility in any other way, which is a key expectation and requires both Banka Slovenije and the European Central Bank. The Constitutional Court recognised Article 350a of the then applicable Banking Act and found in its 120th paragraph of the Constitutional Decision that this is a specially regulated liability of the Bank of Slovenia. Therefore, neither the Government nor the legislator can propose a different form of responsibility, as this would constitute a retroactive intervention into the legal bases in force at the time of the expiry. We also believe that the solutions proposed do not conflict with the rules on monetary financing, since the possible payment of compensation does not constitute a monetary intervention by the Bank of Slovenia in favour of the State. Banka Slovenije has made deletions as a body for monitoring and resolution of banks. Its responsibility is based on then applicable law and European regulation. The last consultations that we conducted last week in the International Monetary Fund, together with the Bank of Slovenia, confirm that the bank resolution authorities are responsible for carrying out burden-sharing measures. This is a good practice recommended by the IMF, based on the principle of credibility, this is the relationship between powers and competences and responsibility for the actions taken. We are also aware of the importance of the wider independence of the Bank of Slovenia, so in designing and supplementing the proposed legal solutions, we have tried to find a compromise solution to the creation of a mechanism to cover possible damages. This mechanism also foresees the important contribution of the Republic of Slovenia, as well as certain safeguards towards as little interference as possible with the general reserves of the Bank of Slovenia. The proposed mechanism set out in Article 40 shall allow for the gradual filling of dedicated reserves that may be the basis for the payment of compensation if they are assessed. Above all, this mechanism protects the budget and taxpayers because the potential liabilities are the responsibility of the Bank of Slovenia and will also pay compensation.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u88	A nice hello to you all! In 2013 and 2014, Slovenia itself tried to solve the huge problem that arose in its banks, avoiding the forced administration of so-called troikas and the actions of the International Monetary Fund, while maintaining a functioning economic system and maintaining financial sovereignty. Its owners also had to cooperate in the resolution of the banks in accordance with the so-called Bail-and-Bail principle. This included those who previously bought securities, known as subordinated bonds, whose issuance has since 2010 been used by banks to raise money for their capital adequacy. When it was found that deep holes were gaping in bank balance sheets, it was decided that the capital paid in subordinated bonds should also be used to cover these losses. At that time, the National Assembly adopted additions to banking legislation allowing Banka Slovenije to issue decisions allowing investors' investments to be fully devalued and converted to bank capital. In 2016, the Constitutional Court decided that such a bank recovery was carried out in accordance with the Slovenian Constitution. It found, however, that the holders of the bonds had a violation of the fundamental constitutional right to judicial protection. The legislation adopted did not allow them to challenge in court the decisions by which the Bank of Slovenia imposed on them the obligation to cooperate in the rehabilitation. Therefore, the Constitutional Court ordered the National Assembly to draw up a law allowing such judicial protection. We have such a proposal finally before us today, ready to vote. Respectable, the expectations of some toward this law are, of course, great. Some believe that the law should return all their contributions to the injured, as it is supposed to be a bank correction for crime directed from abroad. But this law only regulates the right of injured persons to seek their right in court. Therefore, this proposal for a law establishes everything necessary for such procedures to finally run out. The following decision of the Constitutional Court provides for a way in which former bondholders will be able to prove their rights. They will be able to do so if they can prove that banks would lose less in bankruptcy than they lost by participating in their resolution. Therefore, access to the data used to calculate the depth of the bank hole, which has never been disclosed to the public and not to the injured, is essential. The bill thus establishes a way of accessing data through the data rooms, with a large part of the law designed to protect privacy and confidentiality in order to satisfy a number of rules that determine the independence of central banks and protect banking secrecy. The proposal for a law was supplemented by the possibility of settling with the smallest investors, for whom, according to their material situation, it is reasonable to assume that they did not cooperate with speculative intent in the purchase of securities. It also took into account the proposal of the judiciary that due to the speed of proceedings under this law, they should be brought before a court in Maribor. Under this law, the defendant is the Bank of Slovenia, which, if its liability is demonstrated, is also obliged to settle any damage. This solution, by the way, is against it. The European Central Bank is also in the same position. They claim that it is a prohibited monetary financing, but we must remember the last years when both central banks referred to their independence mainly when it was necessary to deposit accounts. This independence was discussed in a way that they were not obliged to explain their conduct to anyone, as if their independence was a goal and not a means entrusted to them because of their important role and responsibilities toward people. Ladies and gentlemen, we must remember that Slovenia has self-sustained its banks, so it did not have to call for the help of the troika. The Bank of Slovenia, which acted as a stand-alone regulator for the banking system in 2013 and 2014. From the very beginning, the Crown Act on Banka Slovenije provides that Banka Slovenije is responsible for the damage caused by its actions. Therefore, her indemnification does not interfere with her independence. ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u96	Thank you very much for your words, Mr. Vice President. Dear Members and Members! The proposal is prepared for the entry into force of the so-called Prospectus Regulation in the European legal order. It is a prospectus regulation which is published when securities are offered to the public or placed on the regulated market, together with the repeal of the previous Directive, which began to apply in the European Union area on 21 July 2019. The Prospective Regulation has been adopted at EU level since the various approaches previously adopted by individual countries when offering or trading in securities to the public on a regulated market and the Prospectus Directive mentioned above have caused fragmentation of the internal market and the differences in the laws of the Member States have significantly hampered the smooth functioning of the Single Capital Market. With the implementation of the Prospective Regulation, requirements for issuers, tenderers and investors for the classification of securities on trade, trading on a regulated market in all Member States are now uniform. Prospects approved in one Member State may also be used in all other Member States. In the EU, uniform disclosure and smooth functioning of the internal market are ensured. Given that the Prospective Regulation is now in force directly, it was therefore necessary to amend the Law on the Market in Financial Instruments, which so far transposed into the Slovenian legal order the Prospective Directive, which was repealed by the Prospectus Regulation. The draft law also eliminates certain uncertainties or inconsistencies in the current provisions of the Law on the Market in Financial Instruments, which have not been detected since its implementation into practice. Since no additional amendments have been tabled in the second reading of the law after consideration in the home working body, I propose that, in accordance with the provisions of the Rules of Procedure, the third reading of the law be carried out at this meeting of the National Assembly. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u106	Say good-bye again! The proposal to amend and supplement the Law on the Market in Financial Instruments is a result of the adoption of a new European regulation in this field. The European rules governing the trade in financial instruments are in the midst of a constant process of unifying that they will apply uniformly and directly throughout the European Union. Until now, the existing rules have been covered by the European Directive, which Member States, including Slovenia, have transposed into their national laws. By adopting a new European regulation, which is directly applicable, many of the provisions of the Directive and on the basis of it, the adopted legislation have become unnecessary. Such a large majority of legislative changes are the deletion of articles that have so far transposed the provisions of the European Directive and the harmonisation of the mutual references of individual articles. As already mentioned, however, the rules remain in force only to be regulated uniformly and at European level in a regulation which applies equally and directly to all members. In its consideration of the draft law, some stakeholders expressed the view that the legal order of this area is thus becoming too complex and less understandable. This can also be agreed upon. Unifying rules at European level is certainly a good thing, but in a complex area such as trade in financial instruments, we want to make the legal provisions clearer. The debate therefore explained that the new rules would have an impact on those trading in these securities. In the SD Group of Members, we will support this bill.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u110	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. Honorable representatives of the government, dear colleagues and colleagues! Already in September 2015, the European Commission published an action plan on the creation of a Capital Markets Union. When we are talking about the Capital Markets Union, it means that certain legalities in the capital market must then work uniformly at the EU level. One former, old 2003 directive allowed the capital market to be regulated by individual countries for themselves. This action plan of the European Commission was followed by a Regulation of the European Parliament and of the Council in June 2017. This Regulation represents an essential step towards the establishment of a Capital Markets Union. And this novel of the Law on the Market in Financial Instruments is also trying to internalise, if I may say so, the regulation of 2017, number 1129. This is a prospectus regulation published when securities are offered to the public or placed on the regulated market. This Regulation, once again, repealed the 2003 Directive. The Directive allowed different rules to apply in different Member States, i.e. the old ones. And, as it is said, if it is a capital market union, then uniform rules must be established in all Member States. Members and Members of the New Slovenia – Christian Democrats will not oppose this law. The new deletes the provisions by which the 2003 Directive was transposed into the Slovenian legal order and is now contained in the 2017 Regulation, i.e. this key regulation establishing the Capital Markets Union. The Regulation provides that exceptions to the obligation to publish a prospectus for certain types of securities offers remain within the competence of Slovenia. The law provides that this exemption shall apply to securities where the total selling price in euro within this tender is less than EUR 3 million within a period of 12 months, b, such tender shall not be required to be notified in accordance with Article 25 of the already mentioned Regulation. Interestingly, in its Article 3, the Regulation sets a ceiling for exceptions to the publication obligation of EUR 8 million. There was a question here that I put on the committee why the government is proposing a lower amount of EUR 5 million than the regulation allows. On the committee I received a relatively satisfactory response, which, of course, points to the dimension of the capital market in Slovenia. We therefore welcome this implementation of the European acquis into Slovenia's legal order and, as it is said, we will not oppose the law. Thank you very much.

Only the practical implementation of the professionally demanding rules shows where anomalies, deficiencies and inconsistencies are occurring and where they need to be addressed. The notice refers to a prospectus published publicly by securities issuers at the time of the offer of the securities to the public or at the time of their entry into trading on a regulated market. The European Directive has already been transposed into the Slovenian legal order. This Directive laid down harmonised principles and rules for the preparation of approvals for the publication of prospectuses and rules for the placement of securities on a regulated market. The Directive has not achieved its effect, as different rules still apply in the Member States of the European Union. This has led to the fragmentation of the internal market in the Member States and thus to the optimal functioning of the internal market in securities. For all of that reason, the Directive was repealed. The provisions introduced into the law on its basis are now deleted and replaced by the provisions of the new Regulation of 2017. Unlike the repealed Directive, this Directive applies directly and uniformly in all Member States. In Alenka Bratušek, as a major change, we recognize the exception to the obligation to publish prospectuses which are within the competence of the Republic of Slovenia and adapted to its market. The threshold allowing an exemption from the obligation to publish is relatively low, but not without justified reasons, in the view of the other Member States. The setting of the threshold depends on the size and development of the capital market in Slovenia. The Securities Market Agency shall be the supervisory authority responsible for the implementation of the provisions laid down by the Regulation and shall also disclose through a transparent procedure information on the measures taken. In addition to the publication of information on measures and infringements, dissuasive sanctions also constitute financial penalties which severely penalise serious infringements. In the Alenka Bratušek Group we will support the amendment of the law. We advocate strengthening confidence in the transparency of markets within the Union, reducing administrative barriers and complexity of procedures that slow and financial burden the market. All of these attract foreign investors to our market on the one hand and, on the other hand, open the door to domestic investors who take part in the European Union market without legal risks and financial burdens. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u116	Thank you very much, Madam President. Dear Members and Members! Other novels of the Law on the Mass Valuation of Real Estate complement the existing regime of mass valuation in a very narrow part of real estate. These are special evaluation units. These include petrol services, power plants and ports and marinas. Although these properties represent a smaller proportion in relation to the number of all properties in the Republic of Slovenia, they are very important. The aim of the novel is to ensure an even more transparent and constitutionally consistent valuation of this part of the property. The novel establishes an essential starting point for mass evaluation and does not change established practices in any way. Under the Law on the Mass Valuation of Real Estates as a Special Standard for Mass Valuation, all properties in the Republic of Slovenia are assessed as closely as possible on their market value. Thus certain generic property values are useful for several purposes, both public and private. However, for tax purposes, the values can be used if determined by a specific law. However, prior to the determination of such use, such as the imposition of a property tax, it is necessary first to ensure the quality of the valuation of the property. However, due to the potential use of these values for tax purposes, certain standards need to be laid down and the legality must be ensured in advance in the mass valuation process. In accordance with the principle of legality in Article 147 of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia, the general regulation of mass valuation is ensured by the rules already in force and specific to the field of specific units of evaluation is intended to ensure respect for the principle of legality. Special evaluation units that have so far not been substantively defined at legal level shall be defined as functionally rounded units, taking into account their characteristics. More clearly, the procedure for dividing the total value of the specific unit of valuation into its individual components is laid down and it removes the uncertainty so far that the procedure for determining specific circumstances as an arrangement against the general value of real estates is also applied to specific units of valuation. For data on the scope of specific units of valuation, their public shall be determined and the basis provided for a new legal framework shall be that details regarding the specific valuation of immovable property may also be regulated by a sub-legislative regulation. The draft law, together with the draft sub-legislative legislation, has been subject to broad professional coordination, including with interested chambers and individual stakeholders carrying out the activity of specific evaluation units. As regards the content, full agreement was reached so that the solutions fully follow the professional standards for the market assessment of real estate. I believe that the implementation of the bill is essential to ensure the constitutional consistency of the implementation of mass evaluation, and I propose that this amendment be supported. These are specific evaluation units where the most economical use is assessed by type of activity. They are, as a rule, a number of properties linked to a functional whole for the operation or operation of a particular activity. This gives them a common market value. The value of such trading-related property is usually a reflection of its ability to generate returns, since facilities are suitable only for a particular type of business activity. Special evaluation units shall be designed only for the purpose of mass evaluation and the data obtained after mass evaluation shall be recorded as the parent data of this record and shall be a business secret. The data is collected only from actual users. The general value of the specific unit of valuation shall be determined by dividing the total value of all immovables into its constituents. This ensures a fundamental principle of mass valuation, which commands that we must ensure an assessment of the market value of each property recorded in real estate records. The proposal for a new Law on the mass valuation of real estate will define in more detail specific evaluation units such as petrol services, power plants and ports or marinas. The definition of these units is particularly important due to the technical performance of the evaluation. This will build on the regulation of specific units of valuation in the property mass valuation system and will address the shortcomings of the legal basis and other uncertainties regarding the regulation of specific units of valuation. ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u130	Your Honor! The law provides for a method of assessing the value of real estate for a specific commercial purpose. These are so-called special evaluation units. These are properties where built infrastructure or equipment determines the most economical use of real estate only for a specific purpose, as a rule for the purpose of carrying out specific activities, and more specifically for petrol services, power plants and ports. This would have a significant impact on their value. In view of the specific properties involved, the law proposes derogations from regular valuation models and the introduction of a specific valuation method. Today we are considering a proposal for a law that speaks only of petrol services, power stations and ports, but because it is a directly related issue which also affects our decision on this law, in New Slovenia we also point to a special, less favourable regulation of the valuation of agricultural and forest land. This is an arrangement where there is a high probability of non-compliance with the constitutional principle of equality as set out in Article 14 of the Constitution. In Novi Slovenije we also submitted a constitutional assessment request on the basis of these arguments. At the time of the adoption of the Law on the mass valuation of real estates, the Legislative and Legal Service on the Special Arrangement for the Valuation of Certain Real Estates stated: “In terms of compliance with the general principle of equality referred to in Article 14, paragraph 14, of the Constitution, the questionable arrangement which agricultural and forest land values by purpose of use, while remaining real estates are valued by actual use, as well as the arrangements which gas services, power stations and ports value according to the size of the activity, while other real estates are valued by the size of the real estate itself.” Therefore, the fundamental regulation of the Law on the mass valuation of real estates does not treat certain real estate owners, including owners of agricultural and forestry land on an equal footing with owners of other real estates. It is a matter of cases where land that is subject to real-use valuation is actually agricultural and forestry, for example, nuts, cuts, fruits and other agricultural activities. However, since they are for the purpose of building, the law stipulates that such land is valued in a manner that is like building land. We are convinced that such an arrangement is inconsistent with the principle of equality before the law referred to in Article 14, paragraph 2, of the Constitution. Since the proposed amendment to the Law on the Mass Valuation of Real Estate does not deviate from this fundamental unequal treatment, we cannot support the proposal.

The new law before us represents one of the steps towards the goal of introducing a property tax, which is a key source of funding for the tasks of the municipalities. Obstacles to the introduction of the tax are mainly unregulated land conditions, urgent caution in determining the model of valuation, definition, complementing and harmonising all missing legal bases. In Alenka Bratušek, we believe that it is extremely important before the tax is introduced that the estimated value of the property is as qualitative and reliable as possible. In April this year, we extended the legal deadline for a new model of mass valuation by April 2020, as constructive cooperation with municipalities and the entire public concerned is essential in the design of the model. During the implementation of the existing law, in particular in the process of professional coordination of evaluation models for specific units, the current regime has proved to be flawed. Special units represent functional units, usually comprising several real estates connected to a functional unit for the purpose of carrying out certain activities. Their market value shall be estimated on the basis of the return. These special units are petrol services, marinas, ports and power plants. The new law provides for a more detailed definition of the definition of these units, which is the basis for an even more detailed regulation with the Specific Evaluation Units Regulation. In the section on the identification of specific circumstances, it is explicitly specified that the identification of specific circumstances also refers to specific evaluation units. In addition, the legal basis is provided for the recording of the real estate market of registered real estate transactions to be taken over from existing real estate records, thus facilitating reporting to this register and facilitating the analysis of the collected data. In Alenka Bratušek we support the proposed arrangement, as the evaluation of specific units is regulated in a more transparent and constitutionally coherent way. Thank you.

While property taxation is more than two decades old, it is a political story under the professional auspices of the World Bank. In Slovenia, this area has long been covered under carpets because government administrations have feared the political resistance of property owners every time. The Slovenian real estate situation is special because it is based on a large imbalance between the value of real estate and the social status of their owners. Much of the private works during the period of socialism were based on inflationary financing with negative capital prices, and in the 1990s the privatisation sale of large amounts of dwellings in the then social property was all upgraded. The quantitative valuation of real estates does not have any other true usable value other than the tax base. Such an evaluation cannot replace a market value that is important for real estate. Similarly, such valuation does not in itself allow for the necessary flexibility required by the taxation of immovable property. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u141	Thank you very much. The following is a presentation of the views of the groups of Members. First of all, Mr Aljaž Kovacič is given the floor to present the position of the LMŠ Group. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-10-22-SDZ8-Redna-12.ana.u142	Thank you for your word, Vice President. Representatives of the government, colleagues and colleagues! Student work is a temporary and occasional work used by a student, student or other eligible person through an authorised organisation on the basis of a travel authorisation. This is a special system of work through travel that no European country knows. Student work developed at the end of the 1950s when students in Ljubljana exported milk and decided to devote some of their earnings to the financing of individual activities of interest. Shortly thereafter, the University Committee of the Union of Students of Yugoslavia adopted an act establishing the first service unit of student service, which we still know today as intermediaries of such works. Temporary and occasional work of students and students in Slovenia, an instrument that enables young people in the education process to gain valuable informal work experience that they cannot obtain in the formal curriculum. Such learning increases the employability of young people and, on the other hand, enables them to obtain financial resources that can afford to pay the increasing costs they have during school. Young people also acquire their first working habits and contacts with potential employers, making it easier for them to enter the labour market. Today, legislation regulates student work in more detail than it did years ago. Much has been done with amendments to higher education legislation, which has considerably limited fictitious entries and thus abused student travel. Records have been set up to prevent effective enrollments, restrictions on chain student status, limitations on study duration and so on. However, legislation in the field of labour market inspection has recently brought about changes. The applicant submitted a proposal for a law to raise the minimum hourly rate for student work to 5 whole euros gross. In the Letter of Marjan Šarc, we have assessed that, despite good intentions, such an increase could cause more harm than good. Any reckless move in this area can be reflected in the extent or offer of work by employers and can cause more work to be done in black. This would be the most damaging for students and students who would have fewer benefits, fewer retirements and less valuable experiences to facilitate their transition to the labour market. By comparison: The 2012 ZUJF, which boosted student work for an additional 13.4%, caused a 22% decline in the industry. This decline, however, did not translate into regular employment for young people, but into inactive ones, as research has also confirmed. Let us not forget the important fact that the country also receives more than €60 million a year from student work through benefits. There are scholarships, student homes, VAT, health checkout and so on. However, the next aspect should be noted. Too high an increase in the minimum hourly rate would have a negative impact mainly on young people in areas outside central Slovenia. Indeed, in Ljubljana and the area, employers are already paying more than the statutory minimum hourly rate, and they are providing more in remote areas with difficulty. For example, in Prekmurje, 60% of the hourly items are below €5 per hour and in central Slovenia less than 30%. In the Group of Members of the List of Marjan Šarc, we called on the relevant stakeholders to draw up the views of the proposed law. The opinions received were different. In the Group of Members of the List of Marjan Šarca we support raising the minimum hourly rates for student work, but we want to be extremely careful in introducing changes in this area due to historical experience. The Institute of Minimum Timetables for Student Work was introduced in 2015 and the amount of that minimum hourly rate was calculated from the minimum wage of EUR 4.5 gross. The same logic or approach was taken by the coalition this time. At the meeting of the parent working body, we successfully tabled amendments proposing that the minimum hourly rate for student work be calculated from the minimum wage divided by the average number of working hours per month. Like the Government, we have also proposed removing the supplements from the marriage text, because we cannot compare student work with the working relationship and other forms of work. We all want a better social situation for young people, but we must also have in mind, in regulatory regulation, an assessment of the possible negative consequences that can be reflected in reality. In the Group of Members of the List of Marjan Šarca, we support the proposed law. The new regulation, which will enter into force on 1 January 2020, will bring additional social security and protection to young people against exploitation in the labour market.

This Decision shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. Regarding the proposal to extend concessions for only one year instead of two years, she said she could not ensure that the conditions for the tendering and award of new concessions would be created by the end of the following year. In this case, apart from the entire planet, I am also concerned about individual cities in our country, specifically the most Ljubljana, where we know that an extremely large number of day migrants are coming, and this is an extremely large number with a personal car. At this point we have 26 companies dealing with this, i.e. 26 separate logistics centres, 26 different schedules, 26 different mechanical operations, 26 separate administrations, and certainly, if we combine all of these into a larger system, all duplications or all of the six-quarters of all these activities that are necessary for you to actually carry out bus services are eliminated. This whole structure is much more rational because it comes under one roof, while such a national carrier is not motivated by profits, but motivated by meeting needs, maintaining quality jobs, servicing communities and so on. Even if profit is then generated, this profit can deliberately return to strengthening quality public transport.
